TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* Why Have Indonesia’s Fires Gotten So Bad?
  Foreign Policy | 30 Sep 05:00 UTC
  This could be their worst-ever year.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: Wildfires are burning vast swaths of rainforest and peatland in Indonesia , Southeast Asian leaders address the U.N. General Assembly , and Bangkok floods displace thousands of people. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: Wildfires are burning vast swaths of rainforest and peatland in Indonesia , Southeast Asian leaders address the U.N. General Assembly , and Bangkok floods displace thousands of people. Indonesia may be on track for its worst-ever year of fires. Wildfires have burned vast swaths of rainforest and peatland in Indonesia in September. The smoke has grown so thick as to drift across borders and oceans to Singapore; Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia; and even Manila, Philippines. The fire season will likely continue until November, according to Suharyanto, the head of Indonesia’s National Disaster Management Agency (BNPB). The dry season this year is particularly long thanks to the advent of El Niño. From January to August, nearly 900,000 hectares of land burned, according to data from Nusantara Atlas, an open-access mapping platform that uses peer review satellite data to survey the damage. This is more than in August 2019, the worst year recorded by that organization. It is possible that 2015 was even worse than 2019, but that’s hard to say definitively. For that year, we have to rely on government estimates , which are hard to compare directly with Nusantara Atlas due to different methodologies. And for 2026, government data so far only goes up to July, so we lack up-to-date estimates. Regardless, this is clearly an extremely bad year. So, what’s causing it? Plantation agriculture is the fundamental underlying cause of the catastrophic forest fires of modern days. As Indonesia’s Kompas newspaper cleverly made clear in its reports this month, fire hotspots overlap almost perfectly with the density of plantations. Plantations worsen the situation in a variety of ways. Clearing and logging build up debris that then serves as fuel. Waterlogged peatland is drained to create plantation land, turning fire-resistant areas into tinderboxes where flames can smolder long underground. And during the dry season, companies and small farmers alike often use fire to clear land to prepare it for planting. But these can easily spiral out of control. Making matters worse this year is the current El Niño, which BNPB head Suharyanto described as particularly
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/30/indonesia-forest-fires-worst-ever-plantations-el-nino/

It helps that the trade teams for the two sides have gotten on surprisingly well since spending time together in Switzerland last year. The big shock of the summit came after the U.S. ambassador to China said that Trump had offered to sell U.S. arms to Xi, apparently thinking it would be heralded as good news despite the fact that it would be illegal under U.S. law and cause consternation among U.S. allies. * Is Egypt’s Diplomacy Clever or Clumsy?
  Foreign Policy | 29 Sep 20:52 UTC
  Regional wars have required Cairo to maneuver between competing demands.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. It has been almost three years since Hamas kicked off a regional war when its cadres and members of other Palestinian extremist groups slaughtered about 1,200 Israelis. In the endless Middle East news cycles since, there has been extensive coverage of Gaza, Israel, Hamas, Lebanon, Hezbollah, Syria, Qatar, Turkey, Iran, Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, and the Houthis, but very little about Egypt. In some ways, it is curious, because Egypt is the most populous Arab country, boasts the region’s biggest military, and is supposed to be the most influential state among members of the Arab League. In other ways, Egypt’s practical invisibility in the past few years makes sense. Egypt is far afield from the region’s battle zones—except for the Gaza Strip—and in the power shift underway in the region, influence has moved eastward from Cairo, Damascus, and Baghdad to Riyadh, Abu Dhabi, and Doha. That does not mean that Egypt has been able to sit out the wars entirely. As these conflicts have dragged on, events have forced President Abdel Fattah al-Sisi into awkward strategic positions and ungainly diplomatic postures. Yet it is not at all clear that this is the result of ineptitude. Egypt is maneuvering between competing and contradictory demands. * Trump’s Global Tariffs Are Likely to Fail in Court (Again)

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. On Wednesday, U.S. President Donald Trump’s third attempt to levy global tariffs will face a challenge in court , after his first two attempts were ruled unlawful, including a landmark ruling earlier this year by the Supreme Court. While the latest batch of tariffs appears to rest on sturdier legal ground than the two that were struck down, they, too, will likely be ruled out of bounds by the U.S. Court of International Trade. At issue is the implementation this summer of tariffs of between 10 percent and 12.5 percent on nearly every U.S. trading partner, ostensibly because all of them—59 countries and the European Union—fail to police the use of forced labor in their supply chains. That failing, the U.S. trade representative surmised , must somehow cause damage to U.S. companies. At the time, trade experts and lawyers saw the move for what it was: a replacement, in a new guise, for the blanket International Emergency Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariffs that the Supreme Court struck down in February. * Don’t Ban Diesel Exports
  Foreign Policy | 29 Sep 13:28 UTC
  Trump’s proposed solution to rising diesel prices would backfire economically and geopolitically.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. As inventories run dry and the Iran war continues to disrupt transit routes, oil has surged back to more than $100 per barrel. Diesel fuel—the workhorse of the agricultural and trucking sectors—has now soared to historic highs, pushing U.S. exports to record levels. With the U.S. midterm elections approaching, Republicans in farm states such as Iowa and Nebraska are demanding relief. In response, U.S. President Donald Trump has endorsed a flawed idea from the 1970s: banning exports. Keeping energy at home to lower prices may seem like common sense, but in reality, this would backfire. If enacted, a diesel export ban would eventually lead to higher prices, discourage investment in supply over time, and undermine the administration’s energy dominance agenda. In response, U.S. President Donald Trump has endorsed a flawed idea from the 1970s: banning exports. Keeping energy at home to lower prices may seem like common sense, but in reality, this would backfire. If enacted, a diesel export ban would eventually lead to higher prices, discourage investment in supply over time, and undermine the administration’s energy dominance agenda. The wars in Iran and Ukraine have squeezed diesel supplies from two of the world’s most important sources. Prior to the war, the Middle East represented more than 15 percent of global diesel exports. Yet attacks on refineries and restrictions on shipping through the Strait of Hormuz have lowered refinery runs by more than 25 percent since last year and thus sharply curtailed refined fuel exports. Meanwhile, Ukrainian strikes on Russian refineries have helped drive diesel production nearly 30 percent below 2025 levels. Russia, normally the world’s second-largest diesel exporter, has restricted exports to conserve fuel at home and announced plans to import diesel to meet domestic demand. * Russia’s Sahel Intervention Is Riskier Than It Looks

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For several hours last month, the fate of the Nigerien junta hung by a thread. At midnight between Aug. 28 and Aug. 29, hundreds of rebel soldiers stormed the capital, Niamey. The attack, which began as an outpouring of frustration over battlefield setbacks and poor welfare benefits, quickly morphed into an attempt to overthrow President Abdourahamane Tchiani, who seized power in the West African state in a coup three years ago. After a 24-hour standoff between the mutineers and government forces, Africa Corps—a Russian paramilitary organization and successor of the now-defunct Wagner Group —came to the state’s aid by dispatching airborne assets , which allowed forces loyal to the junta to regain control. The attack, which began as an outpouring of frustration over battlefield setbacks and poor welfare benefits, quickly morphed into an attempt to overthrow President Abdourahamane Tchiani, who seized power in the West African state in a coup three years ago. After a 24-hour standoff between the mutineers and government forces, Africa Corps—a Russian paramilitary organization and successor of the now-defunct Wagner Group —came to the state’s aid by dispatching airborne assets , which allowed forces loyal to the junta to regain control. For years, Russia has drawn criticism for using counterterrorism deals to prop up client state regimes, including those of former Sudanese President Omar al-Bashir and Central African Republic President Faustin-Archange Touadéra . This year alone, the Africa Corps helped Mali’s junta thwart a large-scale jihadi offensive on the capital city, Bamako; in Niger itself, the group was instrumental in foiling terrorist attacks in January and June . Russia’s role in Niger’s failed mutiny marks the first time that the Africa Corps has intervened to save an African regime from an internal rebellion. Russia’s presence in West Africa has evolved from a security partnership to a literal praetorian guard for hire, ensnaring Niger in a dependency trap that could transform the country into a de facto Russian protectorate instead of a sovereign state. * The True Meaning of Russia’s Sham Election
  Foreign Policy | 29 Sep 09:56 UTC
  Even rigged elections used to be an outlet for discontent. That avenue is now closed.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. What passes for elections in Russia these days bears little resemblance to any recognizably democratic process. Opposition figures are jailed, exiled, or dead. Remaining candidates are carefully screened. In Russian-occupied parts of Ukraine, ballots are cast at gunpoint. Some precincts’ vote tallies are mathematically impossible, and most Russians know that the outcome is predetermined. But in a dictatorship, even a rigged election has meaning. What matters is not the predictable victory declared by the ruling party, United Russia, following the Sept. 18-20 parliamentary elections but the signals that this completely orchestrated piece of political theater sends across Russia and to the world. The first signal is the illusion of legitimacy. By adhering to constitutionally mandated electoral cycles, an autocrat asserts his legal claim to power—never mind that Russian President Vladimir Putin’s mandate from the last more or less genuine election in 2000 has long expired. Putin is very fond of recalling his studies to become a lawyer, and one of the words he has used most often in all his public speeches is “law.” In the process of building his dictatorship, Putin always thought to keep the legal fabric intact. On the surface at least, procedures should be followed—a sham legality we know from other dictatorships, including the Soviet Union and Nazi Germany. * Turkey Is Turning Its Breadbasket Into an Oil Hub
  Foreign Policy | 29 Sep 09:00 UTC
  As wars disrupt traditional export routes, Ankara is moving quickly to capitalize—at the expense of the last standing heartland of the Fertile Crescent.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Across Cukurova, the alluvial plain in southern Turkey that lines the easternmost inlet of the Mediterranean Sea, farmers till land so fertile that it bears three harvests a year. Rivers feed cash crops of cotton, corn, wheat, sunflowers, rapeseed, and soybean; the muggy climate warms greenhouses filled with tomatoes, bananas, and avocados; and the mineral-rich soil supports a wide array of produce—citrus, peanuts, lemons, olives, pomegranates, watermelon, and more. It is also, however, a key node in a newly vital route for oil otherwise blocked by the wars in Ukraine and Iran. The Fertile Crescent’s last great expanse of farmland spared by centuries of drought, war, and mismanagement is now vulnerable as Turkey moves to accommodate some 850,000 extra barrels of oil per day, with plans for much more. Across Cukurova, the alluvial plain in southern Turkey that lines the easternmost inlet of the Mediterranean Sea, farmers till land so fertile that it bears three harvests a year. Rivers feed cash crops of cotton, corn, wheat, sunflowers, rapeseed, and soybean; the muggy climate warms greenhouses filled with tomatoes, bananas, and avocados; and the mineral-rich soil supports a wide array of produce—citrus, peanuts, lemons, olives, pomegranates, watermelon, and more. It is also, however, a key node in a newly vital route for oil otherwise blocked by the wars in Ukraine and Iran. The Fertile Crescent’s last great expanse of farmland spared by centuries of drought, war, and mismanagement is now vulnerable as Turkey moves to accommodate some 850,000 extra barrels of oil per day, with plans for much more. Existing oil and industrial facilities have already displaced many of Cukurova’s farmers and fishers. But the expansion underway could complete the plain’s transition from an area that can still live off its arable land and abundant waters—now a luxury in the drought-strained region—to an energy shipping and petrochemical hub. Not only could these industries nearly replace farming; they could permanently alter the land and water that sustain it, making it unfeasible for it to ever return. A worker harvests cotton in the Cukurova district of Adana, Turkey, on Sept. 12, 2019. * Why Nobody Can End the War in Ukraine
  Foreign Policy | 29 Sep 07:35 UTC
  It’s in everyone’s interest to stop the fighting. That’s still not enough.
  FULL TEXT: Understanding the conflict four years on. It’s been over a year since I wrote anything lengthy about the war in Ukraine, and I regret the omission. Other events have captured my attention, alas, even though the conflict is a continuing tragedy and is still casting a significant shadow over events elsewhere. Our fearless leader Ravi Agrawal’s recent account of his visit brought my attention back and led me to reflect on where the conflict is today and what might be done to bring it to an end after nearly five years. Anyone who approaches this topic should do so with humility, for no one has gotten everything right. I didn’t think Russian President Vladimir Putin would invade in 2022, for example, because I thought such a step would be a blunder and leave Russia worse off in the long run. I still think I was right about that, but obviously wrong about Putin’s calculations. Since then, legions of policymakers and pundits have repeatedly failed to see where things were going, ping-ponging between unrealistic optimism and doom-and-gloom defeatism. And the people in charge are no better: Putin miscalculated in thinking the war would be over in a few days, the Biden administration misjudged the effectiveness of sanctions, and Ukraine’s leaders erred when they launched a disastrous counteroffensive in the summer of 2023. U.S. President Donald Trump told Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky that he didn’t “have the cards” and pressed him to cut an unfavorable deal, only to be surprised when a combination of European support and Ukrainian innovation and heroism turned the tide back temporarily. But as Ravi noted, the fortunes of war seem to be shifting back toward Moscow as winter approaches. Wise readers will therefore take everyone’s analysis and advice with a degree of skepticism—including mine. * Cboe's New S&P Deal Opens the Door to Tokenized Options
  Decrypt | 29 Sep 21:16 UTC [also carried by: CoinDesk]
  The renewed agreement preserves Cboe's exclusive rights to S&P 500 index options through 2051 while opening the door to pairing the flagship benchmark with blockchain infrastructure.
  FULL TEXT: Cboe Global Markets and S&P Dow Jones Indices are exploring whether to bring options contracts on-chain, tucking a tokenization ambition into a 25-year extension of their decades-old licensing partnership. The two firms said Monday they had renewed their exclusive licensing agreement through 2051, preserving Cboe's rights to offer options on the S&P 500 Index, or SPX, its flagship product. The partnership dates to 1983, when Cboe launched the first S&P 500 index options. Beyond locking in that arrangement, the companies said they may collaborate on new products "beyond traditional index derivatives," specifically naming tokenized options contracts. The tokenization piece is exploratory, not a launch. Any tokenized contract would be a distinct product from the SPX options trading today, and the firms framed the possibility as an opportunity to pair a marquee traditional-finance benchmark with blockchain infrastructure. SPX options are a heavily traded market, reaching a record 970.6 million contracts in 2025, according to figures cited alongside the announcement. Cboe shares rose more than 6% after the news. The move plants one of derivatives trading's biggest names in a corner of crypto that has been drawing a rush of institutional interest. Tokenization, the practice of issuing traditional assets as blockchain-based tokens, has moved from concept to competition over the past year. The NYSE recently tapped Blockchain.com to reach crypto investors with tokenized stocks and ETFs, while BlackRock has leaned deeper into the space through a tie-up with Ondo Finance. A consortium including BlackRock, Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan and the DTCC has separately explored tokenized stocks. The timing is notable. The exploration follows the SEC's recent " innovation exemption ," which opened a compliant pathway for tokenized U.S. stocks to trade on-chain without registering as national securities exchanges, part of a broader regulatory shift toward accommodating the technology after the Clarity Act stalled in Congress. Options are a more complex instrument to tokenize than stocks, carrying expiration dates, strike prices, and settlement mechanics that a tokenized version would need to handle. For now, Cboe and S&P DJI have signaled intent rather than a product, but the pairing of a flagship index franchise with on-chain rails marks another sign of tokenization's march into mainstream finance.
  https://decrypt.co/379619/cboes-sp-deal-tokenized-options

* How France’s Religious Nationalists Hijacked the Pope’s Visit
  New Lines | 29 Sep 14:12 UTC
  From far-right billionaires to ultraconservative TikTok influencers, a who’s who of France’s identitarian Catholics played a role in the pope’s first visit to France since his election, and left Easter eggs all along his route for their followers to see. The post How France’s Religious Nationalists Hijacked the Pope’s Visit appeared first...
  FULL TEXT: Chants of “Léon, Léon” rang out as Pope Leo XIV walked onto the stage of the Stade de France in Seine-Saint-Denis on Friday. This, said Monseigneur Etienne Guillet, bishop of Seine-Saint-Denis, was the “stadium of all France,” highlighting the cosmopolitan, multiethnic and multicultural character of his diocese just northeast of Paris — and of the 80,000 young people gathered there. Behind Leo stood a giant cross, and, nearby, the Holy Tunic of Argenteuil, said to be stained with the blood of Christ. “Let us look at the two relics before us,” the pontiff said, inviting France’s Catholic youth to meditate on the Passion, a message of divine love, hope and grace. But to those who knew to look for it, the two emblems on the stage of the Stade de France carried another message — this one not addressed by Leo but planted there by followers of France’s version of Christian nationalism, known as identitarians, who treat Christianity as a badge of ethnic and cultural belonging in their supposed battle for the preservation of white European identity. The giant cross that stood behind Leo during the Stade de France vigil was provided by SOS Calvaires, an association that restores roadside crucifixes, or calvaries. But behind their heritage work is a decidedly identitarian project. In 2022, the investigative outlet StreetPress revealed that among other far-right ties, one of SOS Calvaires’s early funders was Pierre-Édouard Stérin, the far-right billionaire hoping to use his financial firepower to remold French politics in line with his conservative ideals. “A calvary is a banner,” the group’s chairperson said, “It’s a flag you plant.” Questioned about the controversial cross, the vigil’s producer said the Vatican had found the original cross too small, and that SOS Calvaires simply happened to have another. The tunic, kept in a basilica in the Paris suburb of Argenteuil, has very little historical grounding; scientific analysis dates its cloth to the Merovingian era, centuries after Christ. Still, its restoration was funded by conservative billionaire Vincent Bolloré, whose media empire, notably his CNews channel (often compared to Fox News), dragged France’s far right from the margins to primetime, and by his friend, Trump ally and Europe’s richest man Bernard Arnault. Arnault, head of the LVMH luxury group, recently had dinner with Marine Le Pen and is now said to be “preparing for the far right’s rise to power.” The cross and the tunic were just two Easter eggs am
  https://newlinesmag.com/spotlight/how-frances-religious-nationalists-hijacked-the-popes-visit/

The experience upends the typical vulnerability and openness of his life: Askey, under court order, cannot speak with his wife about the random violence heaped upon him, yet nevertheless worries about what his infant son “received from me” during his wordless days in the courtroom. He must withstand the gruesome details of the criminal trial, yet will not be allowed to participate in the jury’s deliberations. His personal essay is a challenging exploration of enforced silence, and the ways in which we seek to unburden ourselves through voice. Before the trial, the other would-be jurors and I were asked our names, our occupations, whether we had spouses or children, and whether we could be impartial regarding the case, in which a man was being accused of murdering his girlfriend. At first I was curious to hear other people’s answers— Oh, wow, that guy fixes airplanes? —but curiosity quickly gave way to numb shock at how many people’s lives had been touched by domestic violence or murder. Or both. People you see every day. People who fix airplanes. One man said that, when he was little, his dad had beaten his mother; if he was being honest, he didn’t think he could be unbiased. The man was dismissed, and a woman was called up from the uncomfortable wooden pews to face the same interrogation. She had been attacked herself, she said. If I were magically able to return to the 1950s and tell Freek about the Khoi who had pastured their stock at the Cape for hundreds or perhaps thousands of years before the Dutch came and laid waste to their society, about a people who were his ancestors, he would have thought it was just a story I was making up. Yemenis, trapped in their own war for more than a decade, yearn for that same release. This September, Yemen’s government tried to borrow Syria’s ending. The Houthis cited those preparations as their reason to strike first. On Sept. 7, Assistant Defense Minister Samir al-Sabri announced that the decision to liberate Sanaa had been taken. By then, the Houthis were already on the move; their west coast offensive had begun on Sept. 4. On Sept. 6, as solidarity posts surged, al-Kadaha in western Taiz fell, severing the road between Taiz and Mokha. At dawn on Sept. 10, government forces slipped out of Mokha, and within a week the Houthis held the coast of Bab al-Mandab.   Lost in a maze or found in a labyrinth? Come, let’s keep walking.
  FULL TEXT: Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Once, as a young adult in the city, I was walking home after a long day of work. The main street leading to my place wasn’t well lit, with dark stretches between streetlamps. There was a huddle of men under one, some standing around, some lounging against the post. They moved in the orange light, turning as one to face me. Something shifted in the air. An earlier version of me would have been slower to respond, given them the benefit of the doubt. But after a recent knife-point mugging, I had begun to develop a sort of second skin—a keen, physical sense of danger. I slipped back into the shadows and ran in and out of alleyways. I doubled back, cut across fences, and scaled half walls. I made no sound at all; I made it home. I had made a spiraling maze in real time. They could not find me. In his essay “ From the Tree to the Labyrinth ,” Umberto Eco, a lover of labyrinths, explores how we “create and organize knowledge.” Moving away from the restrictive “tree” model, Eco approaches the “labyrinth” model, listing three kinds—a unicursal labyrinth, with only one path to the center and back; a multicursal maze, with many paths, all but one leading to dead ends; and an infinite network of interlinked points, which he calls by the borrowed term “rhizome.” There are many labyrinths and mazes woven into my memory—verbal, textual, graphical, physical, metaphorical. Tamil kolam s , for instance: My mother draws small, perfect threshold patterns at home, and immense, geometrically flawless labyrinths and mazes for events—no outlines, no tools, just her seeing eye and moving hand. When the sky is still dark, she dips folded muslin in wet, ground rice and draws liquid lines on the clean floor. She must draw each bit before the white dries on her hand, which it does almost instantly. Nothing can be erased or redrawn. She must keep moving until the whole kolam is done—the body walking the labyrinth even as it summons it out of nothing, the labyrinth making the world even as it is devoured by little black ants. Eco’s rhizome model, to its credit, says we may never know everything, but we can keep going. Jorge Luis Borges certainly did, over and over like a modern-day Scheherazade. * Don’t use the ‘C-word’
  Aeon | 29 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A cancer diagnosis carries with it fear and upheaval. For many patients the cellular changes do not warrant the label - by Matthew R Cooperberg Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Light micrograph of a section through a prostate gland with benign prostatic hyperplasia. Photo by Steve Gschmeissner/SPL William came to me for a third opinion. He was 53 and healthy, with a wife and two teenage children, a busy job, and a typically hectic work-and-family schedule. He’d taken time to do the right thing and had visited his primary care provider for a routine health exam and blood tests, his first in many years. A few weeks later, he’d found himself in an unfamiliar urologist’s office, at the edge of an abyss, ears ringing with what may be three of the most dreaded words in medicine: ‘You have cancer.’ This diagnosis portends pain, suffering and death: in the United States alone, cancer kills more than 600,000 people a year, enough lives to empty a city. Yet, receiving a cancer diagnosis doesn’t necessarily mean what it once did. William was in very broad company: more than 2 million Americans a year receive the diagnosis and, for many, the finding entails little to no threat to length or quality of life, except through the ill effects of treatments. However, patients labelled with the ‘C-word’ face substantial clinical, psychological, social and economic consequences. This disconnect – between what the word evokes in my exam room and what it often describes in contemporary practice – drives controversies and variability in screening practices, and in light of our growing understanding of cellular biology, demands that we rethink what we really mean when we apply the label ‘cancer’. William’s cancer was in his prostate, an organ of whose existence he was previously only vaguely aware, but whose removal or radiation risked impairments to urinary, bowel and sexual function. His biopsy had revealed a prostate tumour we designate ‘grade group 1’, which features few molecular hallmarks of cancer and no immediate potential for metastasis or death. Nonetheless, beset with many competing opinions from his rapidly expanding medical team, William found himself rushing headlong toward treatment, and wrestling with a constant anxiety he’d never felt before. By the time he reached my office, he had taken medical leave from work and confided that, perversely, he was smoking for the first time in decades. W hen I started medical school in the 1990s, I found cancer fascinating and terrifying exactly because of the betrayal it represented. Most human disease represents failure of one body system or another. Whether due to trauma, infection, degradation of b
  https://aeon.co/essays/we-need-a-better-way-to-describe-what-is-often-called-cancer

* Oh, How Lucky We Are
  Longreads | 28 Sep 17:48 UTC
  "Sandy West believed that embracing what we cannot understand is the pathway to enlightenment, and that time on her beloved Ossabaw was the best way to learn that lesson."
  FULL TEXT: Beryl Gilothwest’s grandmother, Sandy “Moose” West, passed away at age 108 in 2021. For The Bitter Southerner , Gilothwest profiles a diminutive woman whose love for Ossabaw, an island off the coast of Georgia, was larger than life. In a world striving for “progress,” West was a staunch advocate for thoughtful preservation; she was a fierce conservationist and environmentalist who embraced the unknown and took pleasure in learning from it. “Sandy believed that embracing what we cannot understand is the pathway to enlightenment. In her mind, spending time in the uncharted wilds of Ossabaw is the best way to learn that lesson. Separated from the mainland by a 30-minute boat ride, the island is deeply remote. Cell service is scant, and there is no wi-fi. You have to fend for yourself out there in a way that is uncommon in contemporary culture, where we always seem to have an escape hatch. It’s been over two years since the Grants Pass decision, in which the court’s conservative majority found that it’s not a violation of constitutional protections against “cruel and unusual punishment” to sanction people for sleeping in public places. That overrode earlier rulings from federal appeals courts in 2019 and 2022, rulings that officials across the country often complained about, saying they prevented them from keeping their streets clean and safe. SK Hynix is deepening ties with the U.S. AI industry, including a $4 billion advanced packaging facility in Indiana, where it broke ground in August. Samsung, too, has been slowly and steadily getting its foot in the American door. Such distance, they say, is good for a child. ‘The father, from his distance, supports, protects, nourishes his child,’ wrote D H Lawrence in 1922: ‘and it is ultimately on the remote but powerful father-love that the infant rests.’ Distance is equally necessary for the father who seeks to make art. ‘There is no more sombre enemy of good art than the pram in the hall,’ wrote Cyril Connolly in Enemies of Promise (1938). This is a deeply self-serving story, of course. It’s nice for the male poet not to have to change diapers. But the argument that unsullied privacy is essential to creativity is tenacious. In Jenny Offill’s novel Dept. of Speculation (2014), for example, the narrator vows never to marry but to become ‘an art monster instead’. But what if domesticity – and specifically parenthood – is in fact generative for artists? What if the cliché of solitary male creativity got it wrong? We may begin with one nursing poet. * Reasoning together
  Aeon | 25 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Jürgen Habermas, the great defender of deliberative democracy, lived up to its demands: he never feared changing his mind - by Emilie Prattico Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jürgen Habermas at the philosophical faculty of Frankfurt University, Germany, in January 1969. All photos by Max Scheler/Süddeutsche Zeitung/Alamy Jürgen Habermas died on 14 March 2026, in the German town of Starnberg, aged 96. Every fall, from the mid-1990s into the late-2000s, he would come to Northwestern University in Evanston, Illinois to teach and give talks, a visit that a dedicated group of students looked forward to every fall term. The year that has stayed with me is 2004, when he taught what he’d spelled out in Between Facts and Norms (1992), his opus applying his theory to democratic politics. Law, philosophy, history, political science: students spanned many disciplines to come watch the famous German scholar every week, dressed in Wrangler jeans and a tweed jacket, expose his theory of deliberative politics in his unmistakable nasal voice, thick German accent, and impeccable English. Max Weber and the Frankfurt School, which were intimate to him, since he’d been Theodor Adorno’s research assistant in the 1950s, came to life in his speech and then in schemas and figures in chalk as he theorised about them. I understood almost none of it at first: words I’d read were coming to life in the room, spoken by their author, and still not resolving into sense. After several lectures, the shape came into focus: this wasn’t just Between Facts and Norms being rebuilt in public, but decades of work arriving at a single place. Since The Theory of Communicative Action (1981), Habermas had been arguing that reason survives in modern life only as something people do together, that is, by giving each other reasons and staying open to being moved by them, rather than by force, money or tradition. Between Facts and Norms was that idea turned toward law and democracy directly: a state counts as legitimate only when its laws could in principle be justified to everyone bound by them, through exactly that kind of open exchange. I’d watched that argument take root in a decades-long intellectual history. Thinking is a practice, it involves owing each other explanations, questions, answers. It is not a fast process, and doing it in public, out loud, in front of a room, is what makes it worthwhile. I attended all of Habermas’s seminars during my time at Northwestern with great interest and, to be honest, a sense of awe. One day, the topic of the weekly seminar was inclusion, and I had argued, with the timid conviction of a green graduate student, that deliberative publ

  FULL TEXT: Audiophiles are sometimes described in terms reserved for cults; a 1956 New Yorker story, referenced in Colin Vanderburg’s exceptional tour of extreme listening, refers to “a remarkably articulate group of zealots” and “an almost mystical reverence for sound.” Vanderburg travels among the believers, tracing the decades-long promise of “consumer audio innovations” to their technological apex: the New York International Audio Show, where audiophiles sample six-figure sound systems in pursuit of a signal as pure as the world they already inhabit. More than anything, I have spent long passages of this earthly life sitting on my ass, listening. I listen to music, but what I hear is sound. Texture, timbre, and tone; clarity and detail; space and depth. And I hear, even more acutely, what thwarts sound. Shrill treble, muddled bass — for me these are worse than peeves. They offend hearing. They diminish the human sensorium. Earbuds repulse me; laptop speakers grate; a tabletop Bluetooth unit is a kind of vandalism. The word audiophile — etymologically, one who “loves to hear” — has a wine-sipping pretension that I hate, but sometimes an ugly shoe fits. Like similarly suffixed psychopathologies, the term suggests a state of warped dependence, of inbent sublimation. I didn’t set out to be this way, but neither have I ever sought help. My symptoms are apparent, their causes obscure. What, after all, do we love when we love sound? “The neurophysiological disorder is characterized by a severe aversion to sound—and the struggle to convince others of the severity of that aversion.” “How techno’s most vaunted architect is still building sonic futures.” “A physics expert and audio engineer may have solved the mystery of a strange noise that rattled an Oregon town in 2016.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/09/24/audiophile-new-york-international-audio-show/

  FULL TEXT: R.101 airship parked in Cardington, UK. Image by the Royal Aeronautical Society In this short video, Bill Hammack, an engineer and professor at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, details the extraordinary making and tragic ending of the British airship known as R101. Conceived in 1924 and first flown in 1929, R101 was intended to fly a regular route from England to India. Hammack uses archival images and his own deep research to trace the engineering and history of this remarkable vessel, which the British government planned as the flagship of a fleet that would criss-cross its then-sprawling empire. Those ambitious plans were abandoned after the airship crashed in France in October 1930, killing 48 of the 54 people on board. Hammack offers a riveting peek into a brief moment in aviation history – one ended for good by the crash of the Hindenburg in 1937 – when it appeared as if commercial airships might become a regular sight in skies across the world. The video accompanies Hammack’s book Fatal Flight: The True Story of Britain’s Last Great Airship (2017), which is available as a free audiobook at his website . I am perched atop several tons of iron, with 80 horsepower at my command. This part of farming is straightforward – even a mechanically challenged tree-hugger like me can manage to depress the clutch, shift into second, and ease the throttle forward. The tractor’s tall, deeply treaded tires churn, revolving into a smooth spin. Everything is spinning: fans, belts, alternator, the driveshaft, axles. I don’t understand the mechanism fully, yet the tractor and chopper and I are propelled forward across the knee-high whiskers of a coarsely bearded cornfield. It is Indian summer in southern Wisconsin. My wife and I and our young son live here on her family’s farm. Both of us are employed off the farm, but on weekends and many evenings I am an apprentice to my father-in-law, David. Today, we are taking advantage of a brief period of warm, dry weather. Winter is approaching, and I could be spending the day beside our woodsplitter – the gasoline-sipping, hydraulically heroic mechanism that makes it possible to put up several cords of firewood quickly each year – but that work can wait until the cool, sunny days of fall are shoved aside by the relentless spin of the earth into darkness, sleet, snow and frozen ground. Managed grazing beef farm in the southern Wisconsin Driftless Area, Cavern Point Farm, Marshfield, Wisconsin.   FULL TEXT: America prides itself on religious freedom, a principle that atheist Nichole Manning passionately believes Texas refuses to extend to her family. Manning joined Christian, Jewish, Hindu and other families in challenging the law as unconstitutional. The 5th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals found the law constitutional in April, so it remains in effect. A petition to review the case is pending before the U.S. Supreme Court. There is a serious possibility that the court, with its conservative majority, will allow the law to stand. But if justices weigh history as heavily as they have in other major cases, the historical record actually gives the plaintiffs a strong argument of their own. Behind the constitutional dispute lies an older question. How should public schools handle religion? Americans have argued for centuries over what role, if any, religion should play in public education and whose religion public schools should emphasize. Whatever the Supreme Court eventually decides about the Ten Commandments, that debate will continue. Existing Supreme Court precedent about Ten Commandments displays is muddled. For many years, the Supreme Court relied on the framework from Lemon v. Kurtzman to decide whether a state had violated the First Amendment’s prohibition on laws “respecting an establishment of religion.” That framework famously focused on avoiding an “excessive government entanglement with religion.” Applying this test, the U.S. Supreme Court found it unconstitutional to use public money to pay for certain expenses at private religious schools, including supplements to teachers’ salaries. In 1980, the Supreme Court used the Lemon v. Kurtzman framework to evaluate a Kentucky law mandating that every public school classroom post the Ten Commandments. In a short opinion, the court concluded that the law violated the First Amendment’s Establishment Clause because the “pre-eminent purpose for posting the Ten Commandments on schoolroom walls is plainly religious in nature.” It acknowledged that public schools could include study of the Ten Commandments and other portions of the Bible as part of a curriculum to help students better understand history or literature.   https://newlinesmag.com/argument/should-texas-schools-display-the-ten-commandments/
